THE GATE. ACT = two or more independent tools agree. That is a fact; fix it. REVIEW = one tool only. That is a lead, not a fact, and it is never auto-fixed.

Greptile found 3 findings where CodeRabbit found 139 on the same estate. A lone finding from a weak tool is noise — and a lone finding from a strong tool is still only a lead. Corroboration is the whole point.
TWO NUMBERS THAT ARE THEMSELVES FINDINGS.
1. Only 45 of 525 pull requests ever received an inline AI review. 480 were merged with zero review. That is what the synthetic full-tree PR (#343) exists to pay off.
2. 515 code-scanning alerts have been dismissed — 322 as "won't fix", which is a risk accepted, not a defect refuted. Every one is listed in §13 with a verdict. To their credit: all 515 carry a written reason. Not one was a silent shrug.

§0 — ACT: corroborated by two or more independent tools

These are facts, not leads. 6 of 256 distinct defects.

§6 — CodeScene (the only tool that reads your GIT LOG)

Project 82581 · job 6934386 · analysed 2026-07-14T21:53+05:30 · 69,328 LoC · 738 files · PR integration Active

Every other tool here answers "is this line of code wrong?" CodeQL, Semgrep, CodeRabbit and ESLint read the code as it is right now.

CodeScene answers a different question: "where is this codebase rotting, and how fast?" It reads the git log. That gives four things nothing else can: hotspots (low health × high change frequency), trend (getting better or worse), change coupling (hidden dependencies — i.e. THE TWO-DOORS CLASS, detected from history), and economics (what the debt actually costs you).

And what it does NOT do, honestly: it will not find a broken regex, a dead P0 rule, a jurisdiction attached to the wrong country, or an inverted compliance polarity. It has no idea what your engine means. The domain gates in §12 found those. Both lanes are required.

6.1 — System health: the gap between these two numbers IS the story

7.2
Average Code Health
(Problematic)
3.7
HOTSPOT Code Health
(Unhealthy)
66
files DECLINING
2
files improving
26
climbing hotspot rank
AND declining
7
predicted to decline
The code you change most is nearly twice as rotten as the code you don't. Average health 7.2; hotspot health 3.7. That is the worst possible shape for a codebase — and it is derived from your commit log, not an opinion.

The economics: hotspots are 1.5% of your files and absorb 24% of your development effort. One and a half percent of the code is eating a quarter of your time.

Composition: Red 20.3% · Yellow 40.7% · Green 39%. Architecture: 2 components — 0 improving, 2 declining. 66 files declining against 2 improving. That is a direction, not a snapshot.

6.2 — HOTSPOTS: low health × high change frequency

The intersection is where bugs come from. A bad file nobody touches is not a problem; a bad file everyone touches every week is.

Read the top three rows again. _adapter.js (60 commits), build.js (106), compliance.js (112) are simultaneously the most-changed files in the estate and the least healthy — 1.30 to 2.08 out of 10 — and all three are getting worse.

Those are the exact three files where every P0 in this session lived: the ghost jurisdiction (compliance.js), the R2-write-before-the-gates bug (build.js), the renderer (_adapter.js / audit-app.js).

I found those by hand, one at a time, over hours. CodeScene pointed at the same three files in one pass, from the commit history alone, without parsing a single line of logic.

6.3 — CROSS-REPOSITORY CHANGE COUPLING  THE BIGGEST FINDING

The engine and the renderer live in two separate repositories — and they are 61% coupled.

Change coupling means: these files keep getting changed in the same commit, even though nothing in the code says they are related. It is a hidden-dependency detector.

Change build.js in the engine, and 61% of the time you must also change _adapter.js in the website. That means the payload contract is duplicated across two repos with no shared schema — which is precisely the class that printed "Sector regulator" to clients on 51% of the catalogue, and the class behind the £17.5M fine that was fixed in the database and never reached the client.

§12 — Domain gates (these found what no marketplace tool did)

The general-purpose tools find code defects. These find domain defects. Neither substitutes for the other.

#GateWhat it foundStatus
DG-01regex-health
1,075 patterns / 671 rules		 19 broken catalogue regexes. THREE were P0 and silently DEAD in production.
UK_GDPR_A13/A13.1.b — dpo[@\\s] → the DPO-contact check was dead
UK_TRADING_STANDARDS/TS2.1 — vat\\s*(no) → the VAT-number check was dead
UK_HMR_2012 — \\bPOM\\b → prescription-only-medicine advertising was dead
UK_FSMA_S21 — FRN ?\\d{6} → the FCA authorisation check was dead

Over-escaped means the pattern COMPILES, RUNS, and matches NOTHING — forever — and reports that as compliance. A confident zero, in production, in the law.		 FIXED + GATED
DG-02rule-polarity 5 rules breached firms for NOT advertising what the regulator PROHIBITS. A firm advertising "THE BEST LAW FIRM — GUARANTEED RESULTS" PASSED ABA Rule 7.1; a firm that said nothing of the kind FAILED. MED_CLAIMS is P1 and fires on any page containing "clinic"/"dental"/"treatment" — the entire healthcare vertical was told it breached its medical board for failing to advertise a miracle cure. FIXED + GATED
DG-03prohibition-calibration 6 prohibitions had no pattern and could never fire. Three were P0, including UK_BOTOX_FILLERS_U18 — a criminal offence in England. Now live, with a negation guard so a clinic that says "we do NOT treat under-18s" is never accused of a criminal offence for publishing its own compliance. FIXED + GATED
DG-04reachability 13 modules / 691 lines of correct, tested, cited legal logic unreachable from the mint. citation-gate.js — the gate built to make an unevidenced monetary claim impossible — had never executed once. FIXED + GATED
DG-05nexus-anchoring /incorporated in/ was anchored to no country at all. 6 of 7 real UK/EU/UAE law-firm footers were judged "established in the United States." Mills & Reeve — a UK firm — was served US ABA professional-conduct rules, the ADA, and US attorney-advertising law. 4 of its 8 compliance findings were jurisdictionally void. FIXED + GATED

The honest verdict: the dismissal hygiene here is better than I expected, and I am not going to manufacture alarm. All 515 carry a written reason — not one was a silent shrug. I pulled the 38 dismissed at HIGH severity and verified the four that touch production code against the live source:

• notify.js stripTags() — verified: it genuinely loops to a fixed point. The dismissal is correct.
• notify.js escMd() — verified: it genuinely escapes the backslash in the same pass. Correct.
• audit-app.js:1190 — verified: it genuinely uses textContent, not innerHTML. Correct.

The one real defect is bookkeeping: that last alert is dismissed as "won't fix" when it is in fact FIXED. The code is safe; the alert state lies. And 160 SRI alerts dismissed as "false positive" in one sweep is a decision that deserves re-examination one by one, not defence in aggregate.

Provenance

Every number above was measured, not estimated. Reproduce with:

StepCommand
harvest code-scanning (all states)node tools/sweep/collect-alerts-full.js
harvest every AI review commentnode tools/sweep/collect-reviews.js
Semgrep, fresh full treesemgrep scan --config=p/default --config=p/security-audit --config=p/javascript --sarif
domain + local analysersnode tools/sweep/collect-local.js · collect-eslint.js
fingerprint → dedupe → Union-Find cluster → numbernode tools/sweep/normalise.js sarif
this reportnode tools/sweep/report.js

Fingerprint = SHA256(path ‖ rule_id ‖ SHA256(snippet)) — never the line number. Lines shift on every edit; the defect does not.
Dedupe = hash map, O(1). Cluster = Union-Find, union-by-rank + path compression, bucketed by (path, category) so we never compare across files.
Number = sort by (severity, corroboration, fingerprint) → deterministic. Same input, same numbers, forever.
